Title: Sir John Chardin's Travels in Persia. Never before translated into English. Containing a most particular account of the religion, government, trade, product, rarities, structures, arts and sciences of that great monarchy; an exact description of the court and city of Ispahan. Volume I
Creator: Sir John Chardin; English translation credited on the title page "By Edm. Lloyd, Esq."
Publisher: London: printed for J. Smith in Exeter-Change in the Strand
Publication Date: 1720 (title page in the OCR, file line 68 of the raw text)
Rights: Public Domain (published 1720; public domain by date under the project's 95-year rule)
Source: Internet Archive item bim_eighteenth-century_sir-john-chardins-trave_chardin-john-sir_1720_1, file bim_eighteenth-century_sir-john-chardins-trave_chardin-john-sir_1720_1_djvu.txt (Eighteenth Century collection, microfilm scan), fetched directly (curl, raw bytes) by the library/source-research thread (Era VII session, second pass, eastern-churches agent), 2026-10-23. The item carries no possible-copyright-status field, no licence and no access restriction. Volume II of the same translation (item ..._1720_2) was fetched, searched and not vendored: it names Armenians only in passing and has no Julfa passage.
Provenance note: Header prepended by the library thread; the OCR text follows after the line of three dashes. The only change to the text is mechanical: the long s (U+017F) was replaced by s, 7,927 occurrences, so that words can be searched. Nothing else altered. Rights basis restated: a London printing of 1720 is public domain by date.
Content note: Volume I of two. An outside witness, after the window: Chardin's stay at Isfahan in the early 1670s (the events dated 1672-73 in the text). The Armenian suburb of Julfa appears in the Introduction and the court narrative: the provost of the Armenians of Julfa, "which is that part of the suburbs of Ispahan where they are settled" (file line about 6540 of this file); the provost and the chief merchants receiving the Muscovite ambassador (about 6955); the clergy of Julfa, "that great suburb of Ispahan, the dwelling-place of all the Christian Armenians", with their patriarch petitioning the chief minister about the taxes laid on the churches (about 7195); the flood between the bridge and the borough of Julfa (about 14810). The scan is a microfilm OCR: words are broken at line ends and margin noise appears; sampled at five points and found readable. Second witness for exact wording until a passage is checked against the page image.

3 * The Introduttion. 


8 


from Const antinaple, gave me a Detail of the 
Campain which the Turks made in Polond ;, 
having the foregoing Lear, with little or no 
Opposition, pass d the Great River Neister, 
they ravag d the finest Provinces, and took 
the famous Fortress of Camineick, which 
was the Bulwark of Poland. I was, among 
other things, inform'd, that the Ottoman 
Army had pass d the Danube, over a Bridge 
five hundred Geometrical Paces long, built 
by the greatest Care and Diligence, and at 
the Expence of the Prince of Moldavia; 
and because the Fabrick did not please the 
Grand Seignior, he depriv'd that unfortu- 
nate Prince of his Principality, and Sen- 
tenc'd him to pay a Fine of a hundred and 
fifty thousand Crowns. 

My Letters from the Indies contain'd an 
Account of the Voyage of Monsieur De 1a 
Hay, Vice-Roy of Madagascar, who set our 
from Roche! with a considerable Squadron, 
in the beginning of the Year 1670. He was 


- sent upon the Memorials of Monsieur Car- 


ron, Director General of the French Com- 
pany, to put some great Desigus in Execu- 
tion, and among others, to Seize Banca, a 
little Island Scituated to the East of Sumatra, 
and pretty near Batavia. This little Isle of Ban- 
ca, which is uninhabited, was not in any Bo- 
dy's Hands before that time.. M. Carron judg d 
it a Place proper to be the principal en 
0 


— — 


— — 


be Introduction. 3 


of the French Company in the Indies; and 
he had formd a Design to seize it by Sur- 
prize: But the Hollanders, who were very 
vigilant and circumspect in regard to the 
Dominion which they had got in those 
Countries, hit exactly upon the Aim of the 
French Fleet, as soon as they saw it was 
equpping. They in vain gave out in France, 
that it was design'd for the Mest- Indies, 
they would not be bubbl'd under that Pre- 
text, They dispatch'd three Advice Boats 


one after another to Batavia, with Orders 


for the Council to take Possession of Banca, 
which was executed even before Monsieur 
De la Hay arrivd in the Indies. His was 
a long Voyage, and to his great Misfortune, 
he put in at Madagascar, where he took it 
in his Head to mike War with the People 
of the 1fhand, at the Sollicitation of the 
French, who were Establish'd there. He lost 
six Months there, and near a thousand Men, 
who might have been better employ'd else- 
where ; for he got nothing by quarrelling 
with the Negroes, but on the contrary, he 
enraged them to such a Degree, that they 
would never after be at Peace, nor keep up 
any Commerce with the French, and at last 
drove them quite out of the Ilan. 
Monsieur De Ia Hay went from Madagas- 
car to Surat, and made a stay there till the 
beginning of the Year 1672 ; and then he 
let Sail from thence with Monsieur Carron, 
a 2 con- 


4 
— 


e . 


contrary. to whose Advice he had receiv'd 
Orders not to Act. The Fleet at that time, 
consisted of six Capital, Ships and four 
Pinks ; they put into Harbour at Goa, on 
the 2 ist of January, and met with the Great- 
Britain, another of the King's Ships, with 
4 | two Pinks. These thirteen Vessels steer'd

During these Negotiations a Sickness

was a burning Fever. The Europeans cali 
the Distempers which they catch at Ceylan, 
the Cinnamon Sickness, because the j | 
Scents of that Wood inflame the Humours. 
Several dy'd of it, but the greater part of 
them recover d, tho they too found them-

soon as they got rid of their Fevers; for 
the Victualling of the Fleet fell short in the 
Month of April, notwithstanding the good 
Management of the Vice-Roy, who order'd 
all the Provisions to be bought np and sold 
amongst them again, not permitting any 
Person to deal with the Country People for 
Provifions, for fear of Waste. The most 
common Meat at Trinc-male, is Wild Oxen, 
yet they eat of it but seldom, and then spa- 
ringly, by Reason of a Property which the 
Flesh of that Animal has very particular to 
itself, and yet more strange and surprizing : 
It ingenders Imposthumations in the same 
Parts, and as painful as those that are con-

is still more particular, is, that there is no

There is one remarkable Incident in this 
port of Monsieur De la Haye's Voyage. He 
ad been inform'd, as I was assurd from the 
King his Master's own Mouth, that he snould 
declare War with the Hollanders in the 
Year 1671. The King told him so at his: 
departure, in the Year 1670: And likewise, 
that he sent him to the [des with no other 
Views than those of this War. But when 
he came to Surat at the end of the Year 
1671, he fourd Letters there that informd 
him, the War had been deferr'd upon some 
important Reasons, but that it was only 
put off for- a little time, and that Notice: 
should be sent him shortly when the Decla- 
ration of War should be made. According- 
ly two Packets were dispatch d to him, in 
August and September, 1671 ; which brought 
him certain Advice, that War would be de - 
clard against the Hollanders the Spring fol ·

lowing. I my self dispateh'd those Packets

which were brought me by Monf. Berrier

was just gone from Surat when those Lees" 
ters arrived there. They were of Opinion 
to send them to him by an Express-Boat, 
which they certainly ought to have done; 
But M. Blot, one of the Directors of the 
Company, imagining there was no pressing 
Busine is in them, faid there was no need of 
being at that Expence ; and that there was 
an Indian Vessel belonging to the Broker of 
the French Company, which- was Sailing 
for the Coast of Malabar, and that they 
might be sent that way. The Spirit of Co- 
vetousness prevail'd, and the Packets were 
given to the Irdian Vessel: But mark the 
fatal Eud of it. The Corsairs of Malabar 
met with the Vessel, took it, and after fix 
Months time, the Packets from the Court 
of France open'd and torn to Pieces, fell 
Into the Hands of the French Merchants on 
that Coast, and were sent back to Surat in 
February, 1673, which was above a Year 
after they had been receiv'd from France. 
There is no doubt to be made, but that, if 
they had been deliver'd in due time, M. 
De Ia Haye would have destroyd the Dutch 
Eleet that coverd Ceylan, which was the 
whole Force the Holland Company had, 
and would afterwards have Conquer d 5

This Lord had been, as I told you before, 
fourteen Months in Disgrace, and during 
that time, there was no Prime Minister at 
all, a thing without Precedent in Perssa ; 
three of the Principal of the Crown acted 
in his Room ; he went from time to time to 
Court, the King having neither Banishd, 
nor so much as forbid him his own Royal 
Presence. The Occafion of this Disgrace 
was, because he would not drink Wine, 
excusing himself always on Account of his 
old Age ; the Dignity of Prime Minister, the 
Name of Cheic, which he bore, and signi- 
fies the same as a Religious, or Saint does 
with us, and denotes a Man Consecrated to 
the strictest observance of Religion, and in 
fine, on Account of the Pilgrimage which 
he made to Mecca, whereby he stood enga- 
ged to a greater purity of Life. The King 
seeing him alone, firm in his Resolution not 
to drink Wine, us'd him often with very ill 
Language; he one time went even so far, as 
to strike him upon that Account, he has 
caus'd full Glass's of Wine to be thrown in 
his Face, pour d down upon his Head, and 
over all his ( loaths, and in his Fits he 
us'd to offer him a thousand Indignities of 
this Nature; but when those Fits were

to be blam'd than his Natural Temper, for

Tis her we must accuse for his rigorous 
Practices against the Chri/tians ; were it not 


for the Transports of a blind Zeal, which 


she inspires into him, the Christians would 
have Reason, as well as the Mahometans, to 
bless his Administration : It is true, even 
these do not all bless it, because he hinders 
the King from going into Prodigalities, and 
from squandring his Treasure, like his Pre- 
decessors; and this is seldom pleasing to the 
Court, which is commonly Poor and Indi- 
gent, when the Prince is not Liberal. This 
Minister was fifty five Years of Age, he was 
of a good Stature, well Shap'd, and his Face 
Handsome, as well as his Make ; he had the 
greatest Advantage of Physiognomy in the 
World, a perpetual Calm, and engaging 
sweetness reign'd in his Eyes, and throughout 
his Countenance, and far from perceiving in 
it any of those Signs of a Mind Occupied, 
and taken up, which appear in that of most 
great Ministers ; one saw shine there all the

Silk, and Gold; in Services of Gold, Silver, 
and China, in Bridles for Horses, Saddles, 
and Housings, and in Gold and Silver ready 
Coin d. When the King was about six 
Spaces from the Door of his Apartment, the 
Prime Minister who waited there to receive 
him, caus d some thousands of Livres, of 
Gold, Silver, and Brass, Coin d, to be 
thrown at his Feet. This Pompous man- 
ner of receiving a Prince, is call'd Pich en- 
das ; Pich signifies before, and endas is the 
Verb, to spread, or to extend. They never 
make use of this kind of Pageantry, but for 
the Reception of the Sovereign, nor cover the 
Streets with Stuffs upon any other Occasion. 
However, tis not improper to observe, that 
they only cover one side of it; the other is 
well Swept and Water'd, and strew'd with 
Flowers, especially when the Place and Sea- 
son will afford them. The Stuffs and the 
Silver which are thrown on the Ground, 
are for the Kings Footmen. Sometimes the 
Lord himself, who makes the Feast, buys 
back the Stuffs : Chiec-Ali- can did the same, 
knowing very well, that they could not fell 
them for near so much, as he order'd to be 
given them. This Method of spreading 
Carpets in the Way for the Passage of Kings, 
and great Princes, is one of the ancientest 
Customs in the East, and most universally 
practis d; they have a Precept or Com- 
mand for this deliver d in the Porant, which

The Songs, this Day were adapted to the 
Re-establishment of the Minister. I saw one 
that was full of fine and witty Turns, the 
Burthen of the Song was this. 0 


Even Sol survey d the spacious Reales of Air, 
To see if be could find another Star. 

A. Star, that like the Polar Star could Reign, 
And long be sought it, but he sought in Vain. 


An ingenious Allusion to the Title of, 
Toon Medave, attributed to the Prime Mini- 
Ker, which fignifics the Pole ot Persia. About

all the Meats: there are high Season d, and

delicate Rag vusts ; whatever is Roasted at 
these Feasts, is commonly stuff d after the 

German rally as wecall it; after Dinner, 
the King walks up and down the Apartments 
and Gardens of the House, or else he Re- 
Poses himself, or takes the Diversion of 
seeing the Horses, of Mounting someof them, 
. drawing the Bow, and such like Exercises; 
he lik wise it he takes a Fancy to it, goes

the Eunuchs of the House Accompany. him, 
and the Master of the House is fo far from 
being Jealous on that score, that he prides 
and glories in it extreamly: Such a Power 
have Prejudice and Custom over the Minds 
of that People, who'being otherwise Jealous 
even of their own Brothers, and won't let 
them set a Foot within their Seraglios ; yet 


are they of Opinion, that no greater Honour 


can ever happen to them, nor any higher 
piece of good Fortune, than when the King 


enters into that Place: The Reason 'they 


give for it, is, that the Persons of their 
Kings are Sacred and Sanctified, in a pecu- 
liar Manner above the rest of Mankind, and 
bring along with them wheresoever they 
come, Happiness and Benediction; you are 
not however to sappose, that when the 
King goes into those Places, any Obscenity 
is committed there; they assure on the con- 
trary, that there never was one Example of 
this kind ; but yet, sometimes the King 
taking a Fancy to the Beauty or Wit of some 
young Woman he sees there, defires her of 
the Master of the House; they are far from 
refusing him, for they look upon it to be a 
great Stroak of Fortune, to have a young 
Creature in the King's Seraglio, by whom they 
may back their Interest, and promote them- 
selves. 8 5

T H E first of July the Comptroller Ge- 
+ neral sent for the Superior of the Capa- 
chins ; it was to ask him after me, and to 
let me know. that he had spoke of me to the 
Nazir, who 'efir'd me to come and see: him 
as soon as | could. that he remembred me 
from the Time of my first Journey thither, 
and that he knew the occasion of this, and 
the Commissions the late King had given me, 
and that he wou'd use his utmost Power to 
procure me a happy success, as far as it was 
consistent with the King's Interest, A

Noon; that was his usual hour of coming 
back from the King. I had a gre t desire to 
carry the Superior of the Capuch uns along 
with me as an Interprerer; fearing least I

from it, and when any body stirs in the pre-

or standing, they immediately say that's a 
Fool or a Frenchman, the reason of it is,

The Nazir, or Superintendant, is call'd 
Negef coulibec. This is an active Lord, Vigi- 
Jant, laborious, as difpatchful as can be, 
and a most excellent Minister. It is impossi- 
ble sufficiently to commend the easiness of 
access to him, and the care he takes to dif- 
patch all manner of Business with the ut- 
most expedition. He was first Master of the 
Houshold, when his Uncle the late Superin- 
tendant died, who leaving no Children be- 
hind him, his post was confer'd upon his Ne- 
phew : His Family is numerous ; He has five 
Brothers, and as many Sons, all Men grown, 
but as yet very indifferently settled in the 
World ; which is some sort of an excuse for 
his insatiable defire after Riches. He takes 
every where where he can do it with priva- 
cy and safety; and it he were not restrain'd 
thro' fear of the King, the World could not 
have prodve d a greater Extortioner : Setting 
a-side that Spirit of avarice, he might be ac- 
counted a very honest Man. 4

*the same Design that Harry the Great, of 
*glorious Memory, had concerted, and re- 
*folv'd upon, in the Year 16c9, and which 
began to be put in Execution by a Merchant 
of Amsterdam, whose Name was Isaac le 
* Maire, and who was a very Able and Mighty 
*Experienced Man, when the' Death of his 
' Majesty put a Stop to it: It is much to the 
King's Glory, that he is desirous of execut- 
ing a Design, form'd by his Illustrious An- 
cestors above fifty Years ago, which, if it 
bad taken due Effect in those Days, had 
rendered France at present Mistress of those 
Places, where the Spices are gathered, and

in the Hands of the Natives of that Country. 
It was in the Year 1615, that this Duteb 
*Catmpiny-appropriated to it self, the Island 
*of Amboyna, where the Cleve grows: They 
did the same with Benda, that produces the 
Tree which bears Nutzregs, and Mace, in the 
Year 1612, and it has since in ten Years 
time, Conquered that part of the Island of 
Ceylan, where the Cinnamon grows, begin- 
*ning from the Year 1635, to the Year 1644, 
inclusively. This Company, with these 
* Spices, carry on such a Trade in the Indies, 
and in Europe, as brings in such {immense 
Gains, that if it were to have no other 
Trade but that alone, it would be sufficient 
to keep and maintain it, as on the Contrary, 
if it were deprived of the Possession of those 
*Spices, it could not even Subsist, much less 
Aggrandize it self, Experience shewing suf- 
*ficiently in the Portuguese, and the _ English, 
that the Commerce of Pepper, Linnen, Silks, 
© Salt Petre, Indigo Druggs, and of all other 
- Things which they bring into Europe, do 
not yield them any considerable Profit. 
„ This makes me conjecture (without Pre. 
Jadice to the Opinion of more able and 
penetrating Persons) that the French Cone 
pany will not be able to make any Profits 
that will be worthy it's Establishment : lt

There is another Inconveniency, which 
* is, that the main part of the Trade must be 
* carried on by Gold and Silver, carried An- - 
nually out of France to the Indies, unless 
*there be a free and open Commerce to China 
and Japan, which is what I would chiefl 
and principally aim at. The Means of bh. 
taining it, is to send an honourable Embassy 
in the Name of the King, to the great Cham 
of Tartary, and King of China, and afterwards 
to the Emperor of Japan. There is a great 
deal of Likelyhood, and much Room to 
* hope, that that Commerce would be obtai- 
ned of them, provided the Envoys carried 
*themselves with Prudence and Sagacity ; 
their Instructions ought to be drawn up with 
good Advice, and a great deal of Attention, 
and they must be very punctually followed,

| long and happy Life, and much Prospe- 
„„ / UE, - 
"Mm" HE many Wars, which my Ancestors, 
 *the Kings of France have made, and 


* the many Victories they have obtain d, as 
well over their Neighbours, as over re- 


© mote Kingdoms, having been succeeded 


* by a great Tranquility, which I enjoy at 
* present ; the Merchants of my Dominions, 
* who Traffick all over Europe, have took 
© the Opportunity to beg of me in the most 
* humble manner, to open to them the way 
* of Voyaging, and of Trafficking in the 
© other Parts of the World, as the other 
Nations of Europe do. Their Supplica- 
* tion was by so much the more acceptable 
© to me, as it is back d both by the Desire of 
the Princes and Lords my Subjects, and 
by my own Curiosity, to be exactly in- 
* form'd of the Manners and. Customs of the 
Great Kingdoms out of Europe, of which 
* we knew nothing hitherto, but from the 
* Relations of our Neighbours, who travel 
to the East. I have therefore resolv'd, in 
* order to gratify my own Inclination, and 
comply with the Requests of my Subjects.

To frame a Judgment of this Letter, ac- 
cording to our Idea's and Manners, there 
is certainly nothing that can be found 
fault with in it; but such is the Polite 
Civility of that other World to which it 
was Addressd, that they found two De-

fegs therein. The first was, its being 
Scald wich a Common Seal. These Sove- 
igns of the East, have Seals of different 
zes ; the largest about the bigness of a 
| Grown-piece, the lefler about tue bigness 
gf à Sixpence, bath the one and the other 
ort of different Figures, as square, round 
oyal ; but the least of all are only put to 
Letters and Orders directed to Persons of 
a lower Rank, or else to Subjects. This 
s been known a long time ago at Vienna 
'enice, Rome, in Poland and Muscouy, by 
reason of their mutual intercourses of 
Trade, All Letters likewise which they 
write out of those Countries to the King 
of Persia, are under the Broad-Seal, and 
that Seal is inclos'd in a Box of Gold ; tor 
it is another piece of Eastern Civility, to is 
put the Letters into rich Boxes, or into 
urses made of Stuffs, more or less. rich, 
in proportion to the Quality of the Per 
Tons to whom they are directed. - 
The second Fault which the Court of 
Pera found with the King of France 
Letter, was, that it w as sent only upon

by two Gentlemen Travellers, whose Cu 
riosity led them thicher, and not by an

These good Capuchin Fathers had taken too

they had nothing to do but to come, that 
the want of Credential Letters, Orders. and 
Instructions, signied but little; because 
that Deficiency should be Supplied, and that

They say in Persia, open the Belly, as we

is not of so quick a Dispatch as is that of 
Beheading.

On the 28th, being at Court, I was told, 
that the Grand Vizier had there receiv'd a 
greater Affront the Night before from the 
King, than all the rest. It was in his Wine, 
as usual. I have already taken notice that 
this Minister is quite white, and very vene- 
rable: He wears short Whiskers, and the 
Beard on his Cheeks, and Chin pretty lon 
because he makes Profession of a strict Ob- 
servance of the Mahometan Religion, which 
teaches, That Decency so requires. But 
the Original Perssaus of Georgia, especially 
those who frequent the Court, and the 
Men of the Sword, on the contrary, wear 
the Beard of the Cheeks and Chin very 
fhort, and their Whiskers so long, that they 
may, for the most part, tuck them under 
their Ear. The King seeing his Minister 
only following another Mode, and the 
Fumes of the Wine disturbing his Brain, he 
immediately ordered his Beard to be shavd

Barber was preparing to execute this odd

that it might be seen. The Barber was un

On the first of August, the Agents of

They who have read the Pistory of the 
last Centuries, know the great Views, which 
the Illustrious Princes of Orange stir d up 
in the Hollander to go to the Indies, the 
Chief whereot was to Fight the Spaniards 
at the Spring head of their Power, thereby 
to take from them, those immense Riches, 
by the means of which they oppress'd the 
United Provinces, and sent Forces every 
Year against them. The Undertaking was 
il a Glorious, Wise, and of great Urniliry : It 
00 Wi show'd pl ain enough, that Money was the 
plain Nerve of War; for as soon as Hain 
saw it self assaulted in those Country's, 
where she neither entertained Armies 'nor 
Fleets, not suspected she should | ever 
be attack'd, she was confounded, and her 
Strength impair'd. The great Advan- 
tages that accrue from the Commerce in the 
Indies, engaged the Hollanders very Strong- 
ly in this Enterprize. This People who 
are Naturally Cunning, and Understanding, 
born to Trade, and have the most Favou- 


rable

rable Dispositions for Trafick, considering 
the mighty Profits they would reap from 
the Indies, if they settl d there, either by 
Contract, or Conquest, employ'dall its Care, 
and exerted its ntmost Efforts therein. One 
may say, that they have succeeded even be- 
yond their own Expectation; for in all 
likelyhood, they did not imagin at first, 
nor indeed for several Years, they should 
ever become the Masters of what is most ex- 
quisite in the East-Indies. This was the 
Reason, that they did not at first Form 2 
Company, They let particular Persons is 
who have a mind to send Ships thither, 
act the best they could; but when they 
became throughly acquainted with the Com- 
merce of the Country, and that they saw 
themselves take Root in the Irdies, they 
united themselves, and form'd that Body 
of associated Merchants, which was call'd 
the East-India Company. lt settled it self 
in Persia, in the Year 1623, and for many 
Years, its Trade was for the most Part, 
only a Trucking or Barter, with the King, 
The Company unladed its Ships into the 
King's Magazines, who took the greatest 
Part of their Merchandize, and gave them 
in Payment Goods of the Country, 3 
amongst other Things, Wool, Tapestry, 
Silks, and Brocards. This interchanging 
became very Burthensome to the Hall anderr.

(e) The Mabomet ant holy Places, are the | 
Cities of Mecca, and Medina, they call them 
Haramjuve Cherefin, that is to say, Sacred 
and Noble. | 
(d) The Original Term is Rzchs-sefid, 
which signifies White-beard They make 
use of that Figure all over Persia, to denote 
the Principal, and most eminent Person of a 
Place, that who governs the rest, as the Fa. 
ther of a Family in his House, a Captain in 
his Company, a Bailif in the Borough where 
he Commands, and the Leader of a Caravan, 
What there is absurd in this Custom, is 
their giving this Title to People who | 
neither have, nor can have a Beard ; as for | 
Instance, the Person that is here menticn'd, | 
who is an Eunuch; but Use leads them till 
farther, for it is given also to Women, and 
Maidens of Quality. This Figure is taken | 
from the great deference the Eastern People 
have ever paid beyond all the rest of the 
World, to old Men. 

(e) The Apartment of the Women in 
Persia is calld Haram, which is to say, 1 
sacred Place, the entrance whereof is inter- 

diced and forbid.

Ser. 48 | | 5 
5 (b) The Republican Government is nn- 
known in Persia, and further on, to the Ex. 
tremity of the World. None but the De. 
spotick Government is known there, and 
they cannot conceive the Administration of 
the Sovereign Power, by a Plurality of 
Persons of equal Authority, nor even that 
holy and happy Power of the Laws, which 
ferves as a Barrier against Tyranny, They 
are accustom'd throughout the East to the 
Yoke of one Man, whose Caprice is a So 
vereign Law, and who does and undoes as 
he himself pleases, without either Reason 
or Sense. The Hollanders, that they may 
not offend these Manners, speak always of 
their Country as of a Monarchy, after the 
Way of other Countries; and when they 
send an Ambassador into Perfa, the Letters 
are made in the Name of the Governor of 
Batavia, or in the Name of the Prince of 
Orange. The first Embassies they sent to the 
Indies, were always in the Name of the 

Princes of Orange. and with their Letters.

I found all the Family of the French Envoy 
busy in delivering to the Burghers of that 
Quarter, the Presents he was to make: 
shall here set down the Order in which that 
is done, The Piskis Naviez, or Receiver 
of the Presents; notifies to the Great Pre- 
volt, and Governor of the City, that be 
must have such a Number of Men, such 1 
Day, in such a Place, to carry the Presents 
of such an Ambassador, The Governor 
sends for the Commissary of that Ward or 
Quarter, and gives him his Orders accord- 
ingly, and the Commissary delivers them 
to the principal Burghers of the Quarter. 
The Perfian Word for Burgher, is Aet-Noda, 
which signifies the Image of God, because 1 
good Head of a Family represents in bis 
House, the Conduct of God in the Uni- 
verse. These Burghers, to the Number of 
Eight or Ten, take a Man out of each Shop 
of the Quarter, or as many as are necessaty. 
and repair, with a Clerk of the Receiver of 
the Presents, to the Ambassador's Lodging,

where they receive his Presents according 
to the Memorial, and deliver them to these 
Bearers. Each Man takes a Piece, and goes 
away. Fifty Men very often are employ'd to 
carry to an Audience, what one Man alone 
might easily carry. This Practice, is to do Ho- 
nour to the Person who makes the Present, 
because it makes him appear the more con- 
siderable; and likewise for the Grandure 
of the King, by reason that the People see- 
ing the Presents that are brought to bim, 
conclude that he is highly esteem'd by Fo- 
reign Nations. The Present is thus kept 
by the Bearers till the next Morning, when 
they repair to the Place which was appoint- 
ed them, each with that Part that was de- 
liver d into his Hands. It sometimes hap- 
pens that the Present remains even eight or 
ten Days in their Hands. One would think 
that in the Confusion that is caus d by a Crowd 
of sive or six hundred Men of the meaner 
Sort, (for sometimes there are so many em- 
ploy'd to carry a Present) something should 
always be lost ; yet that never happens, and 
the Account is always found exact. It 
is a thing impossible in Persa to rob the 
King ; and, as the Persians say, the Sea it

coming, but he runs away; the Beast per-

Jo Bull, is because the Lyon being the Hiero- 
d W glyphic of the Kings of Persiaz the Astro-

The second Spectacle was that of the Ca.

with that Dexterity and Gracefulness, that 
even charm'd all the Beholders,

largely, as well as most of the Grandees, 
I ask'd one of the Noblemen there present 
the Reason thereof. He answered me, 
That it was out of Grandure, and the bet-

Ma jesty; and then smiling, he told me 
furcher, That it was still kept in Memory, 
what one of his Country- men had done in 
1 solemn Audience, which he had of the 
late King, I presently defir'd to be inform'd, 
what that was. He told me, That in the 
Year 64 two Myscovite Extraordinary Am- 
bassadors, at the Audience the King gave 
them, drank so excessively, that they quite 
lost their Senses: The King drank their 
Master s Health, and would needs have them 
pledge it in a Cup that held about two 
Pints : The second Ambassador not being 
able to digest so much Wine, had a pressing 
Inclination to vomit, and not knowing 
where to disembogue, he took his great 
Sable Cap, which he half fill'd. It is well 
known, that the Myscovites wear large and 
bigh Caps. His Collegue, who was above 
him, and the Secretary of the Embassy, 
Who was below him, enrag'd at so foul an

time, the Companions of this filthy Muses 
uite were forcing him by dint of Blows with

was not at all angry; he only broke up the

Muscovites were the Yusbecs of the Franc, 
He thereby intimated, That as among the 
Mahometans, there is no Nation so nally, 
so meanly educated, nor so Clownish as the 
Tusbecs, (who are the Tartars along the 
River Oxus) so among the Europeans, there 
was not any that equal'd the Myscovites in 
those toul Qualities. |

From the Place whence the King look d 
into the Square, it was impossible for him 
todiscern any Thing of these Presents. The 
King's of Persia are so accustom'd to receive 
Presents, that they don't vouchsafe to look 
at them. The Ministers tell him from what 
Place the Present comes, and of what it 
consists; and when the King ask to see 
any particular Thing, it is sent into the Se- 
ralio, or tothe Place the King appointe. 

To speak the Truth, it is out of Pride 
that they receiye the Presents at so great a 
Distance, and with so much Indifferency ;

Upon the 224, a Price was set on the 
Presents of the Envoys ; it is the Custom in 
Persia, to carry the Present that is made to 
the King, -into a large Appartment of the 
Royal Palace, which is call'd Chiracone, that 
is to say, the House of Wine, because it 
is the Buffet, and the Magazine where all 
the Wine for the King's drinking is kept. 
The Presents are consignd to the chief of 
the King's Buttery, who is the Superinten- 
dant of that Apartment; the Price is set there- 
on some Days after according to the Valua- 
tion of the Merchants, and those that are 
best skill d therein. Each part of the Pre- 
sent is afterwards distributed to those of the 
King's Officers, who have the charge of 
Things of the same Nature, The Tapistry 
for Example is delivered into the Magazine 
of the Place, where the Royal Manufa- 
Quure thereof is practis d; the Arms and 
Cannon are put in the Arsezal. The Jew- 
els are laid up in the Treasury, and so of 
the rest, The particular Intendants of each 
respective Apartment, enter the same into 
their Books, The Present is likewise Re» 
gisterd in the Chamber of Accompts be-

On the 28th, I went and acquainted the 


French and English Envoys, that I had put 


their Letters into the Persian Language by 
the Order of the first Minister. The Exglisb 
Envoy show'd himself very glad thereat, and 
thankd me, - assuring me, that the Emglish 
Company would hold it self very much 
oblig'd to me on that Account. In Reality, 
he had reason to be pleasd that I had made 
the Translation thereof, because 1 had pre- 
serv'd to the Original all its Force and Ener- 
gy, which is what the People ot the Coun- 
try dare not do, for fear of drawing upon 
themselves the Indignation of the Ministers, 
by saying something that might Displease, 
tho' they only do it in Obedience to Com- 
mand. As for the other, I could discover 


through his Thanks, that he was very sorry 


I had seen his Letters, because it was impos- 
fible for the Forgery not to be plain and ob- 
vious to the Eyes of a Frenchman.

On the Second, I went in the Morning to 
the Chiracone, which is the King's Side-Board, 
to see it pack'd up for the Journey, The 
Intendant or Overseer thereof, who is call d 
in Persia Chi-rachi-bachi, which is to say, the 
chief of the Purveyors of Wine, was so 
kind as to show me all the finest Things that 
he had in his keeping. It consisted of seve- 
ral Sets of Dozens of Spoons, of Vessel, 
Cups, Salvers, Dishes, Basons, large Tan- 
kards, Water-pots, Boats, Bottles, Spitting- 
ts, all which were of Gold, either ena: 
mel d, set with precious Stones, or carioully 
garnish'd with Pearls; there is nothing there 
but fine Gold, either delicately wrought, ot 
finely set. It is incredible, the vast Quanti- 
ty, and the Value of this Plate ; there are 
Cups so large, that one cannot hold them in 
one Hand when they are full. There ate 
also Drinking · Cups made like Ladles, which 


are frequently us d at the King's Table, and 


are call'd Azer-peche, that is to say, a thou- 
sand Chimera's. This is to express, that one 


is so drank when one has taken off sors of 
them,

Gold and precious Stones, as I have already 
said. This Lord gave me a Dinner, and 
made me drink of several sorts of Wine and 
Brandy, so much, that my Head turn d 
round in a quarter of an Hour; for those 
Wines are mighty strong, and the Brandy's 
still more Violent. If the Brandy is not as 
strong as Spirits of Wine, it does not please 
in Persia, and the Wine that is most esteem d 
there, is that which is most Intoxicating, 
and fuddles soonest, He treated me like a 
Persian, thinking it was entertaining me fins 
ly, to make me drunk presently, Wine in 
the Persian Language is call'd Cherab, a Ter 
which in its Etemology denotes all forts of

This Duty is fifteen per Cent. by Constitu-

with rich Brocards of Gold and Silver ; and

It is not to be believ'd, the vast Expence 
the King of Perssa is at in these Presents; 
the Number of Garments he thus bestows is 
infinite ; his Wardrobes are therefore always 
kept full of them, and the Nazir causes them 
to be deliver'd according to the King's Plea- 
sure, They are kept in separate Magazines 
according to their respective Sorts. The 
Nazir only marks upon a Ticket, what Ma- 
gazine the Garment which the King gives is 
to be taken out of. The Officers of these 
Magazines and Wardrobes ha'e a settid Duty 
paid them out of these Cloaths, which a- 
mounts to above half the value thereof, 
This Duty or Fee is the chief Perquisite of 
these Officers, and when the King commands 
any Habit to be given without taking Fees, 
(which very rarely happens) he makes them 
good to the Officers, so that they never 
loose them. It is the same in all the Pre- 
sents the King makes; if it be in ready Mo- 
ney, the Super-intendant of the Treasury 
takes five per Cent. which is shar'd among 
several Officers of the King's Houshold. The 
Nazir has for his particular Share two per 
Cent. it it be of Horses, the Master of the 
Horse has the like Fee out of it; if it be 
of Jewels, the chief of the Goldsmiths has 
the same, and so of the others; to ny” +

being Bastinado'd on the Soles of his Feet, 
which is the usual Punishment in that

The Chicane of the Perseans, is as per- 
lex d and intricate as ours, and the Terms 


quit makes use of, are as difficult to reduce into 
15 plain unequivocable Sense; nay, it is 


ore, because their Law being writ in Ara- 
lick, their Procedures are full of Arabick Ex- 
pressions, all particular to the Subject, and 
ery hard to Explain. The Attestations or 
Testimonies are all compos'd in Terms, and 
n Characters as particular as Cyphers. There 

this moreover, that the Letter of their 
'rocesses is quite different from the other; 
othat to learn it, costs the Persians as much 
"ans and Time, as to learn to read any 
_ oreign Character.

The Introduction. 


ey — 
Id — — re 


(a) It is the Custom throughout the East, 
in pecuniary Acts. {simple Notes as well az 
others) to put after the Sum z that half of 
it is so much, ard it is frequently added, 
that the quarter part is so much. The Pep. 
sian⸗ say this is done to prevent Frauds, it be- 
ing easie to change one Word, or one Fi- 
gure, but not many different ones. 

(b) One of the 12 Tears of the artificial 
Period, which the Tarters make use of, | 
have amply treated thereof elsewhere, 

On the 9th of December it began to Rain 
in this City, the Rain continn'd four Days 
successively, It seldom Rains at [spabar, 
even in Winter; but when it does, it Rains 
so hard, and so continually, that the Earth 
is penetrated therewith to above three Foot 
deep, and that is whet moistens it so well. 

On the 23d, fell another Rain, ac- 
company'd with such furious Storms, that 
I never saw the like. It lasted four and 
twenty Hours, and filld with Water not 
only the Streets, but also the Houses and 
Gardens, It damag'd a great many Houses, 
and flung down several Walls. It so swell'd 
the River, that it overflow'd and beat down 
part of the Houses on the Key; it broke 
into that fine Alley, which is the Place tor 
taking the Air at Ispaban, between the 
Bridge and the Borough of Julfa, and rose 


The Introduction. 261 


to the height of four Foot. The Gardens 
thereabouts were laid under Water, and the 
Houses of Pleasure were overthrown. As 
all the Walls of Ispahan are made of Bricks 
of Earth, work'd up with Straw cut small, 

and dry'd in the Sun, it is but laying Water 
to the Bottom of the Wall to make it tum- 

ble down; if it lies there only four and 

twenty Hours, the whole or part is sure to 

fall, unless it be very chick. The Damage 

causd by this Storm, amounted to above 

two Million; the King's loss alone came to 

a hundred thousand Crowns, Two Days 

after the Water was all gone off, and in two 

Days more there was no fign thereof. The 

Ground about Ispaban drinks the Water like 

a Sponge; four drops sokes it, and a quar- 

ter of an Hours Sun or Frost, dries it up in- 
tirely. 


The End of the First Volume.
